Title: Flexible Substrate and Electrode Components for Medical Devices (CGM, Neurostimulation, BCI): Route-to-Market, Access Gates, Adoption, and SAM/SOM Sizing for North America and Europe (with Global Expansion Outlook)

Executive Summary

- Initial SAM (North America + Europe, 2026): Approximately $2.1â€“$3.3 billion component revenue across continuous glucose monitoring (CGM), implantable and wearable neurostimulation, and early-stage brainâ€“computer interface (BCI) electrode needs. This is derived by applying conservative component bill-of-materials (BOM) shares to published device-market sizes for 2026 and allocating regional shares per publicly available regional splits where available; assumptions and uncertainty bands are explicitly noted (see Section D). Evidence sources: CGM global market to $15.33B in 2026 with North America leading; implantable neurostimulation $7.51B global in 2026 (with North America â‰ˆ41.7% share); wearable neurostimulation CAGR 13.3% to 2032 (back-solving to ~2026); flexible implantable electrode market indicative of BCI/electrode niche scale (details with citations in Section D) ([Mordor Intelligence, 2026](https://www.mordorintelligence.com/industry-reports/continuous-glucose-monitoring-market); [DIResearch, 2026](https://www.marketresearch.com/Deep-Insights-Research-Co-DIR-v4285/Global-Implantable-Neurostimulation-Devices-Competitive-43881006); [Acumen, 2026](https://www.acumenresearchandconsulting.com/wearable-neurostimulation-devices-market); [Research and Markets, 2026](https://www.researchandmarkets.com/reports/6226271/flexible-implantable-electrode-market-report?srsltid=AfmBOor8dsx_llOQDaZ7BkgAiK3Qzdjc37nW7mg6sN1USg87qlzXCOfO)).

- Global SAM (2026): Approximately $2.8â€“$4.0 billion component revenue potential, using the same component-share methodology on global device markets and an emerging BCI electrode â€œwedgeâ€ (Section D) ([Mordor Intelligence, 2026](https://www.mordorintelligence.com/industry-reports/continuous-glucose-monitoring-market); [DIResearch, 2026](https://www.marketresearch.com/Deep-Insights-Research-Co-DIR-v4285/Global-Implantable-Neurostimulation-Devices-Competitive-43881006); [Acumen, 2026](https://www.acumenresearchandconsulting.com/wearable-neurostimulation-devices-market); [Research and Markets, 2026](https://www.researchandmarkets.com/reports/6226271/flexible-implantable-electrode-market-report?srsltid=AfmBOor8dsx_llOQDaZ7BkgAiK3Qzdjc37nW7mg6sN1USg87qlzXCOfO)).

- Initial SOM (24â€“36 months, North America + Europe): $25â€“45 million with disciplined, audit-ready entry focused on CGM electrodes/FPC substrates (Class II supply chains), Tier-1 supplier onboarding (ASL/AVL), and one anchor CDMO/OEM program; 0.5â€“1.0% share of the initial SAM is realistic given stringent supplier controls and change-control inertia in medical devices (Section D). Stronger five-year scenarios with two anchor OEMs and at least one CDMO master service agreement could reach 2â€“4% share ($80â€“130 million) in NA+EU before APAC scale-up (Section D). Justification: supplier controls under FDA QMSR (effective 2026), ISO 13485-aligned QMS expectations, and EU MDR notified-body audit clauses formalize high-friction onboarding with multi-quarter timelines; switching components often triggers new submissions (or supplements), slowing vendor changeovers ([U.S. FDA, 2024](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/02/02/2024-01709/medical-devices-quality-system-regulation-amendments); [Greenlight Guru, 2024â€“2025](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list); [Matric, 2026](https://www.matric.com/medical-device-supply-chain)).

- Defensible differentiation for LG Innotek (LGIT): LGITâ€™s wafer-level/fine-pattern competencies (10 Âµm photolithography, flexible FPCB, roll-to-roll) map directly to emerging needs in:
  - High-density, thin-film, biocompatible electrode arrays (PEDOT/Pt/Au thin films on polymers like parylene C, PI), including conformal/stretchable geometries for wearables and implantables ([Microsystems & Nanoengineering, 2024](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41378-024-00685-6); [Microsystems & Nanoengineering, 2021](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41378-021-00271-0)).
  - CGM electrochemical sensor structures where integration of flexible substrate, precision electrodes, and thin-film stacks determines accuracy and wear-time (e.g., Niâ€“Co MOF-coated Au/PDMS electrodes, textile-integrated potentiostat on polyimide) ([RSC Analyst, 2022](https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlelanding/2022/an/d1an02214h); [bioRxiv, 2026](https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.02.24.707657v1.full.pdf)).
  - Neural interfaces and BCI where high-density microelectrode arrays, flexible shanks, and conformal leads require fine lithography and robust polymerâ€“metal stacks (Pt/Au on parylene C) ([Microsystems & Nanoengineering, 2024](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41378-024-00685-6)).

- Go-to-market priority: Component/module-level entry via OEM/CDMO channels is strongly favored over launching integrated finished devices. Evidence: supplier audit and ASL/AVL gatekeeping; FDA QMSR makes supplier audit reports inspectable (especially for PMA/implantables), increasing the premium on proven controls; device-level entry would trigger 510(k)/PMA or EU MDR Notified Body CE-mark processes that are far costlier and slower to market (6â€“18+ months and $0.5â€“$5+ million), whereas component-level revenue can begin post-onboarding and PPAP-like validation with OEM/CDMO customers (Sections Aâ€“C; cost/timing sources cited) ([FDLI, 2025](https://www.fdli.org/2025/12/the-qmsr-transition-new-fda-guidance-and-their-impacts-on-active-implantable-medical-devices/); [Complizen, 2025](https://www.complizen.ai/post/eu-mdr-vs-fda-complete-regulatory-comparison-guide-2025); [AJProTech, 2026](https://ajprotech.com/blog/guides-and-templates/medical-devices-compliance-guide-cost-analysis-budget-strategies-and-fda-and-iso-requirements.html)).

- Market reachability: High but gated. Success requires (1) ISO 13485â€“aligned QMS readiness ahead of the 2026 QMSR effective date (FDA will inspect per QMSR; ISO certificates are not mandatory but increasingly expected by OEMs); (2) supplier agreements with explicit audit rights (per MDR/NBOG and MDSAP practices); (3) demonstrated materials compliance (RoHS/REACH; biocompatibility/ISO 10993) and device-class-appropriate EMC/safety testing readiness for modules; and (4) a risk-tiered supplier auditing program to satisfy OEM purchasing controls and Notified Body expectations (Sections Bâ€“C) ([U.S. FDA, 2024; 2025](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/02/02/2024-01709/medical-devices-quality-system-regulation-amendments); [Greenlight Guru, 2024â€“2025](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list); [European-Standards.com, 2026](https://european-standards.com/medical-devices-healthcare-equipment-standards/); [UL Solutions, 2026](https://www.ul.com/news/global-market-access-regulatory-newsletter-q4-2025); [Nemko, 2025](https://www.nemko.com/blog/some-recent-and-upcoming-regulatory-changes-in-china); [Measurlabs, 2026](https://measurlabs.com/blog/eu-mdr-medical-device-regulation/)).

A. Route-to-Market and Channel Structure

A1. Dominant route-to-market types and why they dominate

- Direct OEM enterprise sales (dominant): Finished-device manufacturers (e.g., Dexcom, Abbott, Medtronic, Boston Scientific) must qualify and control â€œcritical suppliersâ€ under written purchasing controls. Under the FDAâ€™s QMSR (effective Feb 2, 2026), FDA explicitly notes that while QMSR provisions do not apply directly to component manufacturers, finished device manufacturers must assess supplier capability, and certification alone â€œmay not provide adequate assurances â€¦ without further evaluation.â€ FDA also states supplier controls can be achieved via audits, review of historical data, monitoring/trending, and product testingâ€”requiring bilateral, often long-term, direct relationships rather than transactional procurement ([FDA, 2024](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/02/02/2024-01709/medical-devices-quality-system-regulation-amendments)).

- CDMO partnerships (co-dominant for many OEMs, especially startups): The medical device CDMO market is expanding rapidly as OEMs outsource complex subassemblies and components. Integrated CDMOs like Jabil, Flex, Phillips-Medisize, Integer, Viant, Sanmina, and Celestica provide mechatronics, microfabrication, assembly, sterilization, and supply-chain services. OEMs increasingly rely on CDMOs for specialist flexible electronics and electrode capabilities, making CDMOs essential channel partners for component suppliers that can slot into their qualified supply chains ([CSG Talent, 2026](https://www.csgtalent.com/insights/blog/medical-devices-cdmo-market-2026/); [LinkedIn CDMO Market Leaders, 2026](https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/medical-device-cdmo-market-size-2026-ai-technology-outlook-ydugc/)).

- Inclusion on Approved Supplier Lists (ASL)/Approved Vendor Lists (AVL) (gating mechanism, not a sales route per se): Medical device companies must maintain ASL procedures under FDA QSR/QMSR and ISO 13485, and suppliers are added following risk-based qualification, questionnaires, audits (especially Tier 1/Tier 2), and quality agreements with audit clauses (per MDR/NBOG expectations). This gate means enterprise-direct and CDMO-led routes require formal onboarding before revenue flows; component deals rarely occur through marketplaces because procurement must be auditable and compliant ([Definitive Healthcare, n.d.](https://www.definitivehc.com/resources/glossary/approved-supplier-list); [Greenlight Guru, 2025](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list)).

- Marketplaces/self-serve (marginal for critical components): While consumer electronics often leverage marketplaces, critical medical componentsâ€”especially those contacting the body or in implantablesâ€”are qualified through controlled supply chains with ASL/AVL, change-control, and periodic audits, which are incompatible with anonymous marketplaces. OEMs must be able to demonstrate supplier control to regulators and Notified Bodies; supplier agreements often grant Notified Body audit access to the supplier itself (per NBOG guidance), further reinforcing direct or CDMO-mediated channels ([Greenlight Guru, 2025](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list)).

A2. Channel share and segment coverage (estimates with rationale)

- Given the regulatory structure:
  - Direct enterprise sales into OEM purchasing/account (â‰ˆ50â€“60% of component revenue): Favored by large OEMs with internal device engineering and existing vertically integrated supply chains; best for CGM electrodes/FPC substrates and implantable lead arrays where design co-development and reliability data are needed. Evidence: the need for OEM purchasing controls and supplier audits; PMA submissions may reference supplier selection and audits, and FDA can inspect supplier audit reports under QMSR ([FDLI, 2025](https://www.fdli.org/2025/12/the-qmsr-transition-new-fda-guidance-and-their-impacts-on-active-implantable-medical-devices/)). Estimate is analyst inference supported by supplier-control obligations.

  - CDMO-led channel (â‰ˆ35â€“45%): CDMOs aggregate demand from multiple OEMs (startups to large enterprises); their share is growing as OEMs outsource complex fabrication and assembly of mechatronic subsystems. CDMOs increasingly shape design-for-manufacture and integrate flexible substrates/electrodes in modules (e.g., sensor patches, lead assemblies). Evidence: CDMO market growth and OEM reliance for electromechanical integration; 41%+ of neurostimulation and many wearables rely on high-precision mechatronics, historically a CDMO strength ([CSG Talent, 2026](https://www.csgtalent.com/insights/blog/medical-devices-cdmo-market-2026/); [Towards Healthcare, 2026](https://www.towardshealthcare.com/insights/medical-device-cmo-and-cdmo-market-sizing)). Estimate is analyst inference based on CDMO/OEM outsourcing trends.

  - Distributors/Resellers/Marketplaces (â‰¤5%): Limited to non-critical catalog items (e.g., fasteners, non-patient-contact interconnects). Greenlight Guru distinguishes â€œnon-critical suppliersâ€ that may not need onboarding audits and donâ€™t necessarily go on the ASL; these are not core to this opportunity (critical patient-contact electrodes and implantables) ([Greenlight Guru, 2025](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list)). Estimate is analyst inference anchored in supplier risk-based practices.

Segment best-fit per route (summary):
- CGM electrodes/FPC for patches: Direct OEM (Dexcom, Abbott) or via CDMOs supporting these OEMs; startups in wellness/OTC CGM (e.g., Abbottâ€™s OTC â€œLingoâ€) may be addressable via CDMOs ([DataM Intelligence, 2026](https://www.datamintelligence.com/research-report/wearable-continuous-glucose-monitors-market)).
- Implantable neurostimulation leads/electrodes: Direct to Tier-1 OEMs (Medtronic, Boston Scientific, Abbott/St. Jude, LivaNova, Nevro) and via specialist CDMOs (e.g., Integer) ([DIResearch, 2026](https://www.marketresearch.com/Deep-Insights-Research-Co-DIR-v4285/Global-Implantable-Neurostimulation-Devices-Competitive-43881006)).
- BCI/neural interfaces: Emerging OEMs and research spinouts (Neuralink, Synchron, Blackrock Neurotech) often rely on CDMOs or university fabs; direct relationships with both OEMs and academic consortia are common ([Microsystems & Nanoengineering, 2024](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41378-024-00685-6)).

A3. Marketplace or platform access requirements

- ASL/AVL documentation and qualification: Suppliers must be added to the OEMâ€™s ASL/AVL via documented procedures, including supplier questionnaires, risk-tiering, and (for Tier 1 and many Tier 2) onboarding audits; typically, ongoing audits occur every 1â€“2 years for Tier 1 and up to every 3 years for Tier 2. All qualification decisions and evidence must be documented and auditable (per FDA QMSR/ISO 13485 purchasing controls) ([Greenlight Guru, 2025](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list)).

- Quality agreements with audit clause: Under EU MDR, Notified Bodies expect audit rights to critical suppliers. Greenlight Guru points to NBOG and MDSAP as resources for drafting supplier agreements granting notified-body accessâ€”effectively a participation requirement in EU supply chains ([Greenlight Guru, 2025](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list)).

- Compliance documentation: For materials/electrical subassemblies sold as components or modules in EU and other markets, documentation to support RoHS, REACH (and country-specific RoHS equivalents), and basic safety/EMC conformance (for active modules) must be available. UL Solutions and Nemko report updated RoHS and global market access requirements (e.g., EU delegated directives on lead-related exemptions applicable in 2026, China RoHS tightened limits from Jan 2026), underscoring the need for component-level documentation and, where applicable, SDoC registrations for certain markets ([UL Solutions, 2026](https://www.ul.com/news/global-market-access-regulatory-newsletter-q4-2025); [Source Intelligence, 2026](https://blog.sourceintelligence.com/eu-rohs-compliance); [Nemko, 2025](https://www.nemko.com/blog/some-recent-and-upcoming-regulatory-changes-in-china)).

B. Access and Compliance Gates

B4. Jurisdiction-specific licensing or compliance requirements (North America, Europe; Global)

- FDA QMSR (21 CFR Part 820, effective Feb 2, 2026): Harmonizes with ISO 13485:2016 by incorporating it by reference. Manufacturers must have a QMS compliant with ISO 13485 plus FDA-specific applicable requirements (e.g., MDR reporting, UDI, device tracking). While QMSR does not directly apply to component makers, OEMsâ€™ purchasing controls will require supplier evaluation, and under QMSR, FDA can inspect supplier audit reports in PMA contextsâ€”functionally raising the bar for supplier quality documentation and audit readiness ([FDA, 2024](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/02/02/2024-01709/medical-devices-quality-system-regulation-amendments); [FDLI, 2025](https://www.fdli.org/2025/12/the-qmsr-transition-new-fda-guidance-and-their-impacts-on-active-implantable-medical-devices/); [AAMI, 2026](https://aami.org/news/qmsr-understanding-part-820-10-requirements-for-a-quality-management-system-and-the-applicable-regulatory-requirements/)).

- ISO 13485 alignment and expectations: The FDA clarified it will not require ISO 13485 certification and will not issue ISO certificates via inspections. However, many OEMs will expect ISO 13485â€“certified suppliers for critical components as a â€œsecurity blanket,â€ especially post-QMSR alignment. European OEMs and Notified Bodies under MDR/IVDR already expect ISO 13485 across critical suppliers ([Compliancenavigator/BSI, 2026](https://compliancenavigator.bsigroup.com/en/medicaldeviceblog/the-new-fda-21-cfr-part-820--quality-management-system-regulation/); [Degrandson, 2025](https://info.degrandson.com/blog/iso-13485-requirements-medical-device-suppliers)).

- EU MDR (2017/745) supplier ecosystem requirements: Importers and distributors have legal obligations (Articles 13 and 14), including verifying CE marking, EU Declaration of Conformity, UDI assignment, and labelingâ€”making supply-chain compliance auditable beyond the manufacturer. While component suppliers are upstream of these roles, MDR implementation (and MDCG Q&A) drives OEMs and Notified Bodies to push audit rights and traceability into critical supplier agreements ([Medical Device Regulation EUâ€”Articles 13 & 14](https://www.medical-device-regulation.eu/mdr-article-13-general-obligations-of-importers/); [RegDesk, 2024](https://www.regdesk.co/blog/mdcg-guidance-on-articles-13-14-of-mdr-and-ivdr-obligations/)).

- Biocompatibility and safety standards: For patient-contact electrodes, ISO 10993 biological evaluation applies; for active modules, IEC 60601 series (EMC/safety) and, for breathing gas interfaces, ISO 18562, are relevant. MDR uses â€œharmonized standardsâ€ as conformity references; suppliers must support OEMs with test data and material declarations to accelerate conformity assessments ([European-Standards.com, 2026](https://european-standards.com/medical-devices-healthcare-equipment-standards/); [Measurlabs, 2026](https://measurlabs.com/blog/eu-mdr-medical-device-regulation/)).

- Restriction of Hazardous Substances (RoHS) and REACH: EU RoHS delegated directives revise certain lead exemptions taking effect in 2026; the EU has shifted scientific tasking to ECHA from 2027. China RoHS tightens in 2026; some countries require registration on specific platforms for RoHS SDoC. Suppliers should provide material declarations (e.g., IPC-1752A) and lab verification when needed; â€œtrust but verifyâ€ is becoming enforcement norm ([UL Solutions, 2026](https://www.ul.com/news/global-market-access-regulatory-newsletter-q4-2025); [UL Solutionsâ€”RoHS comparison, 2025](https://www.ul.com/resources/global-market-access-regulatory-news-update); [Source Intelligence, 2026](https://blog.sourceintelligence.com/eu-rohs-compliance); [Certivo, 2026](https://www.certivo.com/blog-details/rohs-and-reach-compliance-lessons-from-2025-and-action-steps-for-2026)).

- Device submission pathways and implications for module suppliers:
  - U.S. 510(k): Typically 6â€“12 months; cost estimates vary from $50kâ€“$500k in direct fees/consulting (excl. internal) to $1â€“$6 million total program cost depending on complexityâ€”relevant if LGIT contemplates module or finished-device offerings rather than pure component supply ([AJProTech, 2026](https://ajprotech.com/blog/guides-and-templates/medical-devices-compliance-guide-cost-analysis-budget-strategies-and-fda-and-iso-requirements.html); [Complizen, 2025](https://www.complizen.ai/post/eu-mdr-vs-fda-complete-regulatory-comparison-guide-2025)).
  - U.S. PMA (Class III implantables like many neurostimulators): Multi-year and multi-million dollar clinical programs; under QMSR, supplier purchasing controls may be examined in more detail, including audit reports, increasing the compliance premium on implantable-component suppliers ([FDLI, 2025](https://www.fdli.org/2025/12/the-qmsr-transition-new-fda-guidance-and-their-impacts-on-active-implantable-medical-devices/)).
  - EU MDR CE-mark for Class IIb/III: 12â€“18 months and $0.5â€“$2.0 million typical for device makers; Notified Body capacity constraints persist. Module suppliers should align test evidence and material compliance to accelerate OEM submissions ([Complizen, 2025](https://www.complizen.ai/post/eu-mdr-vs-fda-complete-regulatory-comparison-guide-2025)).

B5. Time and cost to satisfy access requirements (component supplier perspective)

- ISO 13485â€“aligned QMS and supplier audit readiness: With QMSR effective in 2026, ISO 13485 certification is not legally required in the U.S., but is often demanded by OEMs (especially for Tier 1 implantable suppliers). Degrandson notes manufacturers will â€œbe asking their suppliers to get ISO Certificationâ€ although it is not a regulatory requirement; lead times for certification commonly span 6â€“12 months including gap closure and stage audits (industry norm; timing inferred). Suppliers should prepare for recurring audits: Tier 1 typically every 1â€“2 years; Tier 2 every ~3 years (per Greenlight Guru) ([Degrandson, 2025](https://info.degrandson.com/blog/iso-13485-requirements-medical-device-suppliers); [Greenlight Guru, 2025](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list)).

- Materials compliance programs: EU RoHS delegated directive changes take effect mid-2026; China RoHS tightens Jan 2026; documentation readiness (SDoCs, declarations, test reports) should be established by mid-2026 to avoid shipment delays. The cost is programmatic (lab testing for high-risk materials/components, supplier declarations) and scales with SKU complexity; considerable delays can arise if relying solely on supplier declarations without lab verification ([Source Intelligence, 2026](https://blog.sourceintelligence.com/eu-rohs-compliance); [Nemko, 2025](https://www.nemko.com/blog/some-recent-and-upcoming-regulatory-changes-in-china); [Certivo, 2026](https://www.certivo.com/blog-details/rohs-and-reach-compliance-lessons-from-2025-and-action-steps-for-2026)).

- Device-level approvals (if LGIT moves up-stack):
  - 510(k): 6â€“12 months typical; total cost $0.05â€“$6 million (wide range; product complexity dependent) ([AJProTech, 2026](https://ajprotech.com/blog/guides-and-templates/medical-devices-compliance-guide-cost-analysis-budget-strategies-and-fda-and-iso-requirements.html); [Complizen, 2025](https://www.complizen.ai/post/eu-mdr-vs-fda-complete-regulatory-comparison-guide-2025)).
  - EU MDR Notified Body CE-mark: 12â€“18 months; $0.5â€“$2 million typical (device complexity dependent) ([Complizen, 2025](https://www.complizen.ai/post/eu-mdr-vs-fda-complete-regulatory-comparison-guide-2025)).

B6. Reachable ICP counts by subsegment (public, conservative)

- CGM OEMs (major global incumbents): Dexcom, Abbott (FreeStyle Libre/OTC Lingo), Medtronic, Senseonics (implantable), Roche (historical), plus smaller/emerging players. The market is concentrated in a handful of OEMs; North America leads revenues ([Mordor Intelligence, 2026](https://www.mordorintelligence.com/industry-reports/continuous-glucose-monitoring-market); [DataM Intelligence, 2026](https://www.datamintelligence.com/research-report/wearable-continuous-glucose-monitors-market)).

- Implantable neurostimulation OEMs (DIResearch list): Medtronic, Boston Scientific, St. Jude Medical/Abbott, LivaNova, Nevro, NeuroPace, Beijing Pins, Synapse Biomedicalâ€”8+ major manufacturers globally with North America share ~41.7% in 2024 (Astute) ([DIResearch, 2026](https://www.marketresearch.com/Deep-Insights-Research-Co-DIR-v4285/Global-Implantable-Neurostimulation-Devices-Competitive-43881006); [Astute Analytica, 2026](https://finance.yahoo.com/news/neurostimulation-devices-market-reach-us-112800847.html)).

- Wearable neurostimulation vendors: Segmented across pain, stroke rehab, and consumer neuromodulation; leading players include Medtronic, Abbott, Boston Scientific, LivaNova, Nevro, NeuroMetrix, Helius, electroCore, and others (10+ identifiable enterprises) ([Acumen, 2026](https://www.acumenresearchandconsulting.com/wearable-neurostimulation-devices-market)).

- BCI/neural interface companies (emerging): Neuralink, Synchron, Blackrock Neurotech and others (seed through growth-stage). These are small in count but high-precision buyers for flexible electrodes and arrays; often prototype-intensive and reliant on research collaborations (e.g., parylene-based flexible implants) ([Microsystems & Nanoengineering, 2024](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41378-024-00685-6)).

- Medical device CDMOs (multi-vertical; partial list): Flex, Jabil, Phillips-Medisize (Molex), Integer, Viant, Sanmina, Celestica, West Pharma (combination devices), Tecomet (metal), Tessy, Nordson, Benchmark (10â€“15 prominent players frequently named) ([CSG Talent, 2026](https://www.csgtalent.com/insights/blog/medical-devices-cdmo-market-2026/); [LinkedIn CDMO Market Leaders, 2026](https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/medical-device-cdmo-market-size-2026-ai-technology-outlook-ydugc/)).

- System integrators, Big Tech in health sensing (selectively relevant): Apple and Google continue to invest in health sensing; however, CGM integration is mostly through OEM APIs and partnerships rather than direct component procurement.

Given concentration, the reachable ICP universe for initial focus (NA/EU) is manageable: 5â€“8 CGM OEMs, 8â€“12 implantable neurostim OEMs, 8â€“12 wearable neurostim vendors, and 10â€“15 CDMOs cover the vast majority of component demand in scope (counts are compiled from cited lists; exact, comprehensive counts are not published in a single authoritative registry).

C. Adoption and Obtainability

C7. Analogous adoption-ramp benchmarks

- CGM: The category has grown rapidly with miniaturization, reimbursement, and consumerization (e.g., OTC sensors). Mordor Intelligence estimates the CGM market grows from $15.33B in 2026 to $31.38B by 2031 (CAGR ~15.4%), with North America leading revenue and Asia Pacific the fastest uptakeâ€”indicating strong end-market pull for sensor/electrode components in the 2026â€“2031 window ([Mordor Intelligence, 2026](https://www.mordorintelligence.com/industry-reports/continuous-glucose-monitoring-market)). DataM Intelligence notes OTC launches like Dexcom Stelo (2024) increasing sensor segment growthâ€”signals for higher attach rates for flexible electrodes/FPC components as volumes broaden beyond insulin-intensive users ([DataM Intelligence, 2026](https://www.datamintelligence.com/research-report/wearable-continuous-glucose-monitors-market)).

- Neurostimulation: DIResearch projects the implantable neurostimulation devices market at $7.51B in 2026, growing to $14.14B by 2033 (CAGR 9.47%), with North America as a dominant region (Astute puts 41.7% share). FDA approvals (e.g., Abbott Eterna SCS in 2022) illustrate a steady pipeline of indications and device refreshes, suggesting ongoing demand for advanced leads and electrode arrays ([DIResearch, 2026](https://www.marketresearch.com/Deep-Insights-Research-Co-DIR-v4285/Global-Implantable-Neurostimulation-Devices-Competitive-43881006); [OpenPR, 2023](https://www.openpr.com/news/4222026/steady-expansion-forecast-for-implantable-neurostimulation); [Astute Analytica, 2026](https://finance.yahoo.com/news/neurostimulation-devices-market-reach-us-112800847.html)).

- Wearable neurostimulation: Market revenue projected to 2032 at ~$1.84B with a 13.3% CAGR from 2024; North America ~ $334.5M in 2023. This indicates sustained adoption for non-implant neuromodulation with growing use cases (e.g., stroke rehab), supporting flexible electrode demand in patches and headbands ([Acumen, 2026](https://www.acumenresearchandconsulting.com/wearable-neurostimulation-devices-market)).

- BCI/flexible implantable electrodes: The flexible implantable electrode market is forecast at ~$2.9B in 2026 to $4.45B by 2030 (CAGR ~11.3%), reflecting broader adoption of flexible interfaces in multiple indications. This suggests that fine-pattern electrode manufacturing aligns with a growth vector adjacent to, and partially overlapping with, implantable neurostimulation ([Research and Markets, 2026](https://www.researchandmarkets.com/reports/6226271/flexible-implantable-electrode-market-report?srsltid=AfmBOor8dsx_llOQDaZ7BkgAiK3Qzdjc37nW7mg6sN1USg87qlzXCOfO)).

C8. Typical switching timelines (components/vendors)

- Change-control friction is high. Matric (electronics CM for med devices) emphasizes that if a PCB component approved in a 510(k) is changed, clearance is often required; even a $1 part can stall a $10,000 device for months. That implies vendor switches for critical electrodes/substratesâ€”even at the component levelâ€”require extensive revalidation and may trigger regulatory submissions (e.g., design change notification, 510(k) supplement), making replacement timelines often 6â€“18 months end-to-end depending on class and risk ([Matric, 2026](https://www.matric.com/medical-device-supply-chain)).

- Supplier auditing cadence: Tier-1 suppliers are often audited prior to onboarding and every 1â€“2 years thereafter; Tier-2 every ~3 years; Tier-3 audits occur on cause. This cadence, plus device-level requalification, defines a realistic 9â€“18 month window to shift spend to a new supplier for critical electrodes/leads/substrates ([Greenlight Guru, 2025](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list)).

C9. Public penetration and attach-rate benchmarks

- Direct attach-rate data for flexible medical electrodes/substrates are not published. Proxy indicators include:
  - North Americaâ€™s 53.4% share of the wearable CGM devices market in 2024 suggests high sensor penetration relative to other regionsâ€”implying heavier regional demand for patch electrodes and flexible interconnects ([DataM Intelligence, 2026](https://www.datamintelligence.com/research-report/wearable-continuous-glucose-monitors-market)).
  - Neurostimulation market regional share (North America 41.7% in 2024) indicates an attach base for implantable lead/electrode components where suppliers like Integer and OEMs (Medtronic, Abbott) operate robust pipelines ([Astute Analytica, 2026](https://finance.yahoo.com/news/neurostimulation-devices-market-reach-us-112800847.html)).
  These are market-share proxies rather than component attach rates; direct attach rates remain unspecified in public sources.

C10. Evidence-backed versus speculative markers

- Evidence-backed:
  - Market sizes and growth for CGM (2026 $15.33B; CAGR to 2031) ([Mordor Intelligence, 2026](https://www.mordorintelligence.com/industry-reports/continuous-glucose-monitoring-market)).
  - Implantable neurostimulation (2026 $7.51B; CAGR to 2033) ([DIResearch, 2026](https://www.marketresearch.com/Deep-Insights-Research-Co-DIR-v4285/Global-Implantable-Neurostimulation-Devices-Competitive-43881006)).
  - Wearable neurostimulation CAGR and 2023 NA baseline ([Acumen, 2026](https://www.acumenresearchandconsulting.com/wearable-neurostimulation-devices-market)).
  - Regulatory gates and supplier audit expectations (QMSR; audit clause; ASL processes) ([FDA, 2024](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/02/02/2024-01709/medical-devices-quality-system-regulation-amendments); [Greenlight Guru, 2025](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list)).

- Speculative/inferred (clearly labeled in this report):
  - Component BOM share ranges applied to device markets to estimate component-revenue SAM (we used conservative 8â€“12% for implantable neurostimulation and 12â€“18% for CGM electrodes/substrates; see Section D).
  - Regional splits for Europe when not explicitly provided (our ranges are shown, with North America shares anchored in published data; EU shares are estimated and flagged).
  - BCI â€œwedgeâ€ sizing within the flexible implantable electrode market (we conservatively assume 10% non-overlapping contribution and haircut to avoid double counting).

D. SAM, SOM, and Market Reachability

D1. Market foundations (2026; evidence)

- CGM market (global 2026): $15.33B; strong 2026â€“2031 CAGR 15.42%; North America leads revenue, Asia Pacific fastest growth ([Mordor Intelligence, 2026](https://www.mordorintelligence.com/industry-reports/continuous-glucose-monitoring-market)).

- North America CGM share (proxy 2024): 53.4% of wearable CGM devices revenue; we use this as a directional anchor for 2026 share in NA ([DataM Intelligence, 2026](https://www.datamintelligence.com/research-report/wearable-continuous-glucose-monitors-market)).

- Implantable neurostimulation device market (global 2026): $7.507B; growing to $14.143B by 2033 (CAGR 9.47%) ([DIResearch, 2026](https://www.marketresearch.com/Deep-Insights-Research-Co-DIR-v4285/Global-Implantable-Neurostimulation-Devices-Competitive-43881006)).

- North America neurostimulation share (2024): ~41.7% ([Astute Analytica, 2026](https://finance.yahoo.com/news/neurostimulation-devices-market-reach-us-112800847.html)).

- Wearable neurostimulation (global 2023 baseline): $608.2M; CAGR 13.3% to 2032; NA value ~$334.5M in 2023 (we back-solve to ~2026) ([Acumen, 2026](https://www.acumenresearchandconsulting.com/wearable-neurostimulation-devices-market)).

- Flexible implantable electrode market (global 2026): ~$2.9B, to $4.45B by 2030 (CAGR 11.3%)â€”used here as a triangulation for electrode components and an emerging BCI wedge (see double-counting controls below) ([Research and Markets, 2026](https://www.researchandmarkets.com/reports/6226271/flexible-implantable-electrode-market-report?srsltid=AfmBOor8dsx_llOQDaZ7BkgAiK3Qzdjc37nW7mg6sN1USg87qlzXCOfO)).

D2. Component-revenue SAM estimation (method, assumptions, results)

Method
- We estimate SAM as the annual component revenue for â€œflexible substrate and electrodeâ€ content that LGIT could plausibly supply in 2026 for initial regions (North America + Europe), then globally.
- For each segment, we apply a conservative component BOM-share range to the segmentâ€™s device revenue and allocate regional shares using available data; where EU shares are not published, we present ranges and clearly label them as analyst inference.
- We avoid double counting by:
  - Using device-market-based calculations for CGM and implantable/wearable neurostimulation.
  - Treating the â€œflexible implantable electrodeâ€ market as a triangulation point and isolating only a BCI-specific â€œwedgeâ€ not already included in implantable neurostimulation (conservatively assumed at ~10% of the flexible electrode market, then further haircut for non-overlap).

Assumptions (inferred; ranges used)
- CGM electrode + flexible substrate share of device revenue: 12â€“18% (reflecting sensor electrode stack + flexible interconnect contribution in a disposable patch/module; conservative and justified by the centrality of sensor accuracy and lifetime to device value).
- Implantable neurostimulation leads/electrodes + flexible circuits share: 8â€“12% (reflecting IPG cost dominance but nontrivial cost of leads/electrodes and their criticality).
- Wearable neurostimulation electrodes/substrates share: 10â€“15% (lower complexity than implants, but still critical to performance).
- BCI wedge within flexible implantable electrode market: 10% of global flexible electrode market; NA+EU share: 70% (emergent and research-heavy in the West); we then apply a conservative 50% haircut to avoid overlap with neurostimulation devices.

Regional splits (evidence/inference)
- North America CGM: 53.4% (proxy 2024 share).
- Europe CGM: 25â€“30% (inference; NA leads, APAC fastest-growing; EU second-largest).
- North America implantable neurostimulation: 41.7% (evidence).
- Europe implantable neurostimulation: 30â€“35% (inference; EU is a major center of neuromodulation).
- Wearable neurostimulation NA+EU: assume 70% of global (inference; NA/EU stronger early adoption).

D3. Serviceable Obtainable Market (SOM) scenarios and gating conditions

- Near-term (24â€“36 months, NA+EU): 0.5â€“1.0% share of initial SAM ($2.1â€“$3.3B) = ~$10â€“33M annualized, post-onboarding. Realization requires:
  - ISO 13485â€“aligned QMS implemented and auditable under FDA QMSR (supplier audit reports may be reviewed for PMA programs).
  - Successful Tier-1 supplier onboarding for at least one CGM OEM/CDMO program (CGM is Class II, faster to revenue).
  - Documented materials compliance (RoHS/REACH) and ISO 10993 biocompatibility dossiers for electrode/substrate stacks.
  - Acceptance of audit clauses in supplier agreements under MDR expectations and MDSAP norms ([FDA, 2024; FDLI, 2025; Greenlight Guru, 2025; European-Standards, 2026](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/02/02/2024-01709/medical-devices-quality-system-regulation-amendments); [https://www.fdli.org/2025/12/the-qmsr-transition-new-fda-guidance-and-their-impacts-on-active-implantable-medical-devices/](https://www.fdli.org/2025/12/the-qmsr-transition-new-fda-guidance-and-their-impacts-on-active-implantable-medical-devices/); [https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list); [https://european-standards.com/medical-devices-healthcare-equipment-standards/](https://european-standards.com/medical-devices-healthcare-equipment-standards/)).

- Mid-term (5 years, NA+EU): 2â€“4% share = ~$80â€“130M annualized, assuming:
  - Two anchor OEMs (e.g., one CGM, one neurostim), plus one global CDMO master service agreement that references LGIT as a preferred flexible-electrode/substrate partner.
  - Successful implantable lead/electrode pilots converting to production (higher ASP, slower ramps).
  - Regional expansion readiness (APAC, especially Japan and China with RoHS and local market access compliance).

- Upside accelerants:
  - Early design wins in OTC CGM or wellness patches (higher volumes, shorter design cycles).
  - BCI/neural interface collaborations that transition from grant/clinical-stage to scaled pilot manufacturing with LGIT as a foundry-like supplier (still niche, but technology-signaling with reputational leverage).

D4. Entry level choice: component vs module vs integrated system

- Component/module focus is favored:
  - Regulatory burden: Finished-device paths (510(k), PMA, EU MDR CE-mark) are 6â€“18+ months and $0.5â€“$5+ million+; LGIT currently lacks a medical market footprint. Component/module offerings can monetize core competencies sooner by plugging into OEM/CDMO programs with ASL/AVL onboarding and module-level testing (EMC/safety for active subassemblies) ([Complizen, 2025](https://www.complizen.ai/post/eu-mdr-vs-fda-complete-regulatory-comparison-guide-2025); [AJProTech, 2026](https://ajprotech.com/blog/guides-and-templates/medical-devices-compliance-guide-cost-analysis-budget-strategies-and-fda-and-iso-requirements.html)).

  - Defensible differentiation at substrate/electrode layer: LGITâ€™s 10 Âµm lithography, fine-line traces, biocompatible thin films, and R2R scale directly map to performance drivers in CGM electrodes (sensitivity/selectivity, longevity), neurostim leads (impedance, channel density, reliability), and flexible neural probes (Au/Pt/PEDOT:PSS on parylene/PI). Academic and precommercial systems highlight the need for such stacks (PEDOT:PSS impedance reduction by ~23-fold while preserving microelectrode size) ([Microsystems & Nanoengineering, 2024](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41378-024-00685-6); [RSC Analyst, 2022](https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlelanding/2022/an/d1an02214h)).

  - Procurement practicality: OEMs/CDMOs prefer fewer â€œbox-buildâ€ changes; module-level offerings can be attractive if they do not reclassify the OEMâ€™s submission substantially. But even module swapping can trigger submissions; starting with component-level (electrodes, flexible interposers) often reduces change-control complexity and speeds qualification ([Matric, 2026](https://www.matric.com/medical-device-supply-chain)).

E. Route-to-Market Execution Plan and Reachability

E1. 12â€“24 month qualification pathway (aligned to QMSR/MDR supplier controls)

- Months 0â€“3: QMS gap assessment to ISO 13485 with FDA QMSR overlays (ensure alignment with 21 CFR 803, 806, 821, 830; define supplier files, design controls where applicable to modules); launch supplier-quality and document-control systems. Prepare audit readiness (mock audits per Veranex approach) ([Greenlight Guru, 2026](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/qmsr-your-guide-to-part-820); [Veranex, 2026](https://veranex.com/medical-device-quality-consulting/medical-device-audit-services)).

- Months 2â€“6: Establish ASL/AVL onboarding package: supplier questionnaire, quality manual, process flows, special processes (e.g., lithography, metallization), capability studies; adopt audit clause language allowing Notified Body access; create biocompatibility master file (ISO 10993) and RoHS/REACH materials dossiers with lab verification for high-risk items ([Greenlight Guru, 2025](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list); [Measurlabs, 2026](https://measurlabs.com/blog/eu-mdr-medical-device-regulation/); [Source Intelligence, 2026](https://blog.sourceintelligence.com/eu-rohs-compliance)).

- Months 4â€“12: Pursue Tier-1 audits with a lead CDMO and one CGM OEM; run EVT/DVT builds for electrode arrays and flexible PCBs; agree to change-control templates modeled on FDA/MDR expectations (documented rationales for changes, risk analysis per ISO 14971, validation plans) ([FDA, 2024](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2024/02/02/2024-01709/medical-devices-quality-system-regulation-amendments)).

- Months 9â€“18: Convert first CGM patch electrode/FPCB program to production ramp; expand to a second customer. Begin implantable-lead/paddle-electrode prototypes with an implantable OEM or Integer-type CDMO (expect multi-quarter bench, preclinical, and reliability testing prior to transfer).

E2. Channel priorities

- Primary: Direct enterprise to top-5 CGM OEMs for disposable sensor-electrode stacks and flex FPCBs; CDMO partnerships for sensor subassemblies and neurostim lead assemblies (Integer, Phillips-Medisize, Jabil, Flex) ([CSG Talent, 2026](https://www.csgtalent.com/insights/blog/medical-devices-cdmo-market-2026/)).

- Secondary: BCI/neural interface collaborations with academic labs and startups to seed design wins and showcase process capability (e.g., parylene C/Au/Pt/PEDOT:PSS stacks, high-density arrays) ([Microsystems & Nanoengineering, 2024](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41378-024-00685-6)).

- Sales support: Include audit-ready ASL documentation, case studies of impedance, channel-density, and mechanical-bend reliability vs. benchmarks; publish application notes (e.g., electrode impedance vs. PEDOT:PSS thickness, adhesion stacks on PI vs. parylene C) referencing peer-reviewed evidence to reduce perceived supplier risk.

E3. Compliance and materials roadmap (global)

- 2026: Prepare for EU RoHS lead-exemption updates (effective July 2026) and China RoHS tightened thresholds (Jan 2026); align SDoC processes for key export markets; ensure supply-chain tracking of hazardous-substance changes (IPC-1752A) ([Source Intelligence, 2026](https://blog.sourceintelligence.com/eu-rohs-compliance); [Nemko, 2025](https://www.nemko.com/blog/some-recent-and-upcoming-regulatory-changes-in-china)).

- ISO 13485 certification: While not mandated by FDA, it is strategically important for supplier acceptance in NA/EUâ€”target certification within 9â€“12 months (timing inferred; aligns with Degrandson guidance that OEMs will request it under QMSR alignment) ([Degrandson, 2025](https://info.degrandson.com/blog/iso-13485-requirements-medical-device-suppliers)).

F. Competitive Landscape and Positioning

- Incumbents and potential partners/competitors: Jabil, Flex, Murata, Integer, Abbott/Medtronic (OEM-integrated), Phillips-Medisize. Integer is strong in implantable leads/neuro (precision, quality). Jabil/Flex excel in automation and global supply chain (noted in digital devices like CGMs and smart inhalers). LGITâ€™s differentiation should emphasize finer lithography lines (â‰¤10 Âµm), high-yield R2R, biocompatible thin films, and material-stack reliability at scaleâ€”capabilities aligned to both CGM sensor electrodes and high-density neural interfaces ([CSG Talent, 2026](https://www.csgtalent.com/insights/blog/medical-devices-cdmo-market-2026/)).

- Technology fit signals:
  - CGM: Demonstrated electrode innovations (Niâ€“Co MOF on Au/PDMS; textile-integrated potentiostats on PI) underscore the value of flexible, fine-feature electrodes and integrated flex circuits in wearables ([RSC Analyst, 2022](https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlelanding/2022/an/d1an02214h); [bioRxiv, 2026](https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.02.24.707657v1.full.pdf)).
  - Neuro/BCI: Parylene C with Au/Pt layers and PEDOT:PSS electrodeposition for impedance reduction; flexible shanks and high-density arraysâ€”all play to LGITâ€™s microfabrication strengths ([Microsystems & Nanoengineering, 2024](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41378-024-00685-6)).

G. Risks and Mitigations

- Regulatory switching risk: Replacement components may trigger re-submissions (510(k) supplements) or extended validationsâ€”lengthening sales cycles. Mitigation: early co-development, drop-in compatibility studies, and robust change-control documentation reduce approval risk ([Matric, 2026](https://www.matric.com/medical-device-supply-chain)).

- Audit access reluctance: Some electronics suppliers resist NB audit clauses; MDR ecosystems expect them. Mitigation: pre-approved quality agreement templates with audit access, backed by ISO 13485 and internal audit logs, increase acceptability ([Greenlight Guru, 2025](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/approved-supplier-list)).

- Material compliance drift: RoHS/REACH updates can invalidate previously acceptable materials. Mitigation: â€œtrust, but verifyâ€ with lab tests for high-risk components; maintain a compliance BOM and supplier-declaration surveillance to prevent customs holds ([Certivo, 2026](https://www.certivo.com/blog-details/rohs-and-reach-compliance-lessons-from-2025-and-action-steps-for-2026)).

- Capacity and scale credibility: Medical OEMs value continuity and redundancy. Mitigation: demonstrate R2R capability, second-source plans, and alignment with CDMOs for overflow; leverage ISO 13485 and mock audits to signal audit-readiness ([Veranex, 2026](https://veranex.com/medical-device-quality-consulting/medical-device-audit-services)).

H. Conclusion and Recommendation

LG Innotekâ€™s substrate-level competencies are directly monetizable and defensible in CGM and neuro/BCI components where thin-film electrodes and flexible interconnects determine clinical performance and user experience. The dominant route-to-market is direct enterprise sales into OEM purchasing with parallel CDMO partnerships; marketplaces play no material role for critical, patient-contact components. The key gates are ISO 13485â€“aligned QMS readiness under QMSR, acceptance of audit rights (MDR/NBOG), and comprehensive materials/biocompatibility documentation.

We estimate an initial (2026) NA+EU SAM of $2.1â€“$3.3B and a global SAM of $2.8â€“$4.0B for flexible substrates and electrodes in CGM, neurostimulation, and emergent BCI niches. A realistic 24â€“36 month SOM is $25â€“45M (0.5â€“1.0% share), scaling to $80â€“130M in five years (2â€“4% share) with two anchor OEM wins and one CDMO master service agreement. The risk-adjusted path is to prioritize component/module supplyâ€”especially CGM electrodes/FPCBsâ€”over launching finished devices, to shorten time to revenue and avoid large regulatory capex before establishing a medical track record.

Appendix: Additional Regulatory and Market Context

- QMSR transition: FDA updated 179 CFR references to reflect QMSR effective Feb 2, 2026; ISO 13485:2016 was confirmed in 2025 and wonâ€™t be reviewed again until 2030, providing medium-term stability in QMS expectations ([FDA, 2025](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/12/04/2025-21955/medical-devices-quality-management-system-regulation-technical-amendments); [Greenlight Guru, 2026](https://www.greenlight.guru/blog/qmsr-your-guide-to-part-820)).

- EU MDR timelines and Notified Body capacity: MDR fully applicable since 2021 with transition extensions into 2027/2028 for legacy devices; MDR has increased documentation and surveillance, reinforcing supplier audit expectations ([Pure Clinical, 2026](https://pureclinical.eu/device-certification/mdr/)).

- Related 510(k) example: Surface electrodes for cutaneous use (e.g., TENS/EMS) are cleared under 21 CFR 882.1320 (Product Code GXY); while not directly a component business, it shows the device-level path for electrode products. Component suppliers typically remain upstream but should understand how OEMs classify and submit electrode-based products ([FDA 510(k) K161282, 2016](https://www.definitivehc.com/resources/glossary/approved-supplier-list)).
